She told me that my manner was just making things a bit tense for everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your constant need to be doing something is a part of who you are and something you like about yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I suppose I've always been a bit of a doer, but... I don't know if I'd say I necessarily like this constant need to be moving around. It's really just what I do. \n\nI like feeling useful, sure, but... well, if it's causing tension with my friends at the center, I can't say I'm exactly thrilled about it. So, no, I wouldn't say it's a part of myself I'm particularly proud of right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself double-checking or making sure things are done perfectly when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't... I don't fuss over making things perfect. Once I get something done or put away, I just leave it be and move on to whatever is next. I'm not really one to go back and double-check things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in charge or have control when you're busy fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel in charge or anything like that. It's more just... I have a lot of energy and I need to do something with it. I'm not doing it to be the boss or to control the situation. I just need to keep my hands busy.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It's more just... I have a lot of energy and I need to do something with it. I'm not doing it to be the boss or to control the situation. I just need to keep my hands busy.\n\nDoctor: Has your tendency to always be doing something been present throughout your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say it has. I remember way back in grade school, I used to get scolded for popping out of my seat all the time. I was always talking over my classmates, and... well, the teachers would always say I was just too lively. And that was long before I was even a teenager. So, yes, it's been a part of how I am for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions are motivated by trying to achieve a certain standard or meet expectations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm not really trying to live up to anyone's expectations or reach a certain standard. It's really just... well, like I said, I just have this restless energy. I do things because I need to keep moving, not because I'm trying to prove anything or impress anybody.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does affect things a bit, yes. It can cause some friction with people. Like, at the senior center, I’ve actually had to go up and apologize to folks after our meetings. I just get so going, I guess... and one of my friends even suggested that I take a smaller role at the sign-up table. She told me that my manner was making things tense. So, yes, it does bother me when it gets in the way of my relationships and makes people uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to avoid making mistakes when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not really worried about making mistakes or getting things perfect. It's more that I just... I just like to keep busy. If I'm fixing something or moving things around, it's just to give my hands something to do, really. It's not about being exact or avoiding errors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your actions are more about being productive or achieving something, rather than just keeping busy?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm retired, so I don't really have any big need to be productive or achieve things anymore. It truly is just about keeping busy. I just... well, I just need to be doing something. It's not like I have a grand goal in mind when I'm puttering around the house or at the center. I just can't seem to sit idle for very long, so I find things to keep my hands and my feet moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is significantly interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned a few minutes ago, it does get in the way sometimes. I don't know if I'd say it causes me severe distress, but it certainly bothers me. Having to go around and apologize to folks after our senior center meetings... well, that's embarrassing. And when my friend told me I should probably step back from the sign-up table because my manner was making everyone tense, that definitely stung. She told me that my manner was just making things a bit tense for everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your constant need to be doing something is a part of who you are and something you like about yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I suppose I've always been a bit of a doer, but... I don't know if I'd say I necessarily like this constant need to be moving around. It's really just what I do. \n\nI like feeling useful, sure, but... well, if it's causing tension with my friends at the center, I can't say I'm exactly thrilled about it. So, no, I wouldn't say it's a part of myself I'm particularly proud of right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself double-checking or making sure things are done perfectly when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't... I don't fuss over making things perfect. Once I get something done or put away, I just leave it be and move on to whatever is next. I'm not really one to go back and double-check things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in charge or have control when you're busy fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel in charge or anything like that. It's more just... I have a lot of energy and I need to do something with it. I'm not doing it to be the boss or to control the situation. I just need to keep my hands busy.\n\nDoctor: Has your tendency to always be doing something been present throughout your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say it has. I remember way back in grade school, I used to get scolded for popping out of my seat all the time. I was always talking over my classmates, and... well, the teachers would always say I was just too lively. And that was long before I was even a teenager. So, yes, it's been a part of how I am for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions are motivated by trying to achieve a certain standard or meet expectations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm not really trying to live up to anyone's expectations or reach a certain standard. It's really just... well, like I said, I just have this restless energy. I do things because I need to keep moving, not because I'm trying to prove anything or impress anybody.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does affect things a bit, yes. It can cause some friction with people. Like, at the senior center, I’ve actually had to go up and apologize to folks after our meetings. I just get so going, I guess... and one of my friends even suggested that I take a smaller role at the sign-up table. She told me that my manner was making things tense. So, yes, it does bother me when it gets in the way of my relationships and makes people uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to avoid making mistakes when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not really worried about making mistakes or getting things perfect. It's more that I just... I just like to keep busy. If I'm fixing something or moving things around, it's just to give my hands something to do, really. It's not about being exact or avoiding errors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your actions are more about being productive or achieving something, rather than just keeping busy?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm retired, so I don't really have any big need to be productive or achieve things anymore. It truly is just about keeping busy. I just... well, I just need to be doing something. It's not like I have a grand goal in mind when I'm puttering around the house or at the center. I just can't seem to sit idle for very long, so I find things to keep my hands and my feet moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is significantly interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned a few minutes ago, it does get in the way sometimes. I don't know if I'd say it causes me severe distress, but it certainly bothers me. Having to go around and apologize to folks after our senior center meetings... well, that's embarrassing. And when my friend told me I should probably step back from the sign-up table because my manner was making everyone tense, that definitely stung. So, yes, it does interfere with my social life and my relationships to some degree.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your constant need to be doing something is something that fits with your self-image?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that, to be honest. I haven't really thought about it in terms of my self-image. It's just... well, it's just how I am. I've just always been someone who needs to keep moving. I don't really sit around pondering if it fits who I am or my identity, it's just what I do every day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F60.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is that, for the past year or so, I just feel like I'm always puttering around. I'm always fixing things, arranging, or volunteering for just one more thing. Even at 87, I... I just have a really hard time sitting down and being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're not doing something?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious. I don't get nervous or... or uneasy about it. I just, well, I just prefer to be doing something. It's not really a worried feeling at all, I just don't care to sit idle.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to always be doing something caused any problems for you or your relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has caused a few issues. Um, over at the senior center, I’ve actually had to apologize to folks after our meetings. And... well, one friend even suggested I take a smaller role at the sign-up table. She told me that my manner was just making things a bit tense for everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your constant need to be doing something is a part of who you are and something you like about yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I suppose I've always been a bit of a doer, but... I don't know if I'd say I necessarily like this constant need to be moving around. It's really just what I do. \n\nI like feeling useful, sure, but... well, if it's causing tension with my friends at the center, I can't say I'm exactly thrilled about it. So, no, I wouldn't say it's a part of myself I'm particularly proud of right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself double-checking or making sure things are done perfectly when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't... I don't fuss over making things perfect. Once I get something done or put away, I just leave it be and move on to whatever is next. I'm not really one to go back and double-check things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in charge or have control when you're busy fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel in charge or anything like that. It's more just... I have a lot of energy and I need to do something with it. I'm not doing it to be the boss or to control the situation. I just need to keep my hands busy.\n\nDoctor: Has your tendency to always be doing something been present throughout your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say it has. I remember way back in grade school, I used to get scolded for popping out of my seat all the time. I was always talking over my classmates, and... well, the teachers would always say I was just too lively. And that was long before I was even a teenager. So, yes, it's been a part of how I am for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions are motivated by trying to achieve a certain standard or meet expectations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm not really trying to live up to anyone's expectations or reach a certain standard. It's really just... well, like I said, I just have this restless energy. I do things because I need to keep moving, not because I'm trying to prove anything or impress anybody.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does affect things a bit, yes. It can cause some friction with people. Like, at the senior center, I’ve actually had to go up and apologize to folks after our meetings. I just get so going, I guess... and one of my friends even suggested that I take a smaller role at the sign-up table. She told me that my manner was making things tense. So, yes, it does bother me when it gets in the way of my relationships and makes people uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to avoid making mistakes when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not really worried about making mistakes or getting things perfect. It's more that I just... I just like to keep busy. If I'm fixing something or moving things around, it's just to give my hands something to do, really. It's not about being exact or avoiding errors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your actions are more about being productive or achieving something, rather than just keeping busy?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm retired, so I don't really have any big need to be productive or achieve things anymore. It truly is just about keeping busy. I just... well, I just need to be doing something. It's not like I have a grand goal in mind when I'm puttering around the house or at the center. I just can't seem to sit idle for very long, so I find things to keep my hands and my feet moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is significantly interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned a few minutes ago, it does get in the way sometimes. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, what brings me in is that, for the past year or so, I just feel like I'm always puttering around. I'm always fixing things, arranging, or volunteering for just one more thing. Even at 87, I... I just have a really hard time sitting down and being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're not doing something?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious. I don't get nervous or... or uneasy about it. I just, well, I just prefer to be doing something. Um, over at the senior center, I’ve actually had to apologize to folks after our meetings. And... well, one friend even suggested I take a smaller role at the sign-up table. She told me that my manner was just making things a bit tense for everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your constant need to be doing something is a part of who you are and something you like about yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I suppose I've always been a bit of a doer, but... I don't know if I'd say I necessarily like this constant need to be moving around. It's really just what I do. \n\nI like feeling useful, sure, but... well, if it's causing tension with my friends at the center, I can't say I'm exactly thrilled about it. So, no, I wouldn't say it's a part of myself I'm particularly proud of right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself double-checking or making sure things are done perfectly when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't... I don't fuss over making things perfect. Once I get something done or put away, I just leave it be and move on to whatever is next. I'm not really one to go back and double-check things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in charge or have control when you're busy fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel in charge or anything like that. It's more just... I have a lot of energy and I need to do something with it. I'm not doing it to be the boss or to control the situation. I just need to keep my hands busy.\n\nDoctor: Has your tendency to always be doing something been present throughout your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say it has. I remember way back in grade school, I used to get scolded for popping out of my seat all the time. I was always talking over my classmates, and... well, the teachers would always say I was just too lively. And that was long before I was even a teenager. So, yes, it's been a part of how I am for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions are motivated by trying to achieve a certain standard or meet expectations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm not really trying to live up to anyone's expectations or reach a certain standard. It's really just... well, like I said, I just have this restless energy. I do things because I need to keep moving, not because I'm trying to prove anything or impress anybody.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does affect things a bit, yes. It can cause some friction with people. Like, at the senior center, I’ve actually had to go up and apologize to folks after our meetings. I just get so going, I guess... and one of my friends even suggested that I take a smaller role at the sign-up table. She told me that my manner was making things tense. So, yes, it does bother me when it gets in the way of my relationships and makes people uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to avoid making mistakes when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not really worried about making mistakes or getting things perfect. It's more that I just... I just like to keep busy. If I'm fixing something or moving things around, it's just to give my hands something to do, really. It's not about being exact or avoiding errors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your actions are more about being productive or achieving something, rather than just keeping busy?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I just can't seem to sit idle for very long, so I find things to keep my hands and my feet moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is significantly interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned a few minutes ago, it does get in the way sometimes. It's more just... I have a lot of energy and I need to do something with it. I'm not doing it to be the boss or to control the situation. I just need to keep my hands busy.\n\nDoctor: Has your tendency to always be doing something been present throughout your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say it has. I remember way back in grade school, I used to get scolded for popping out of my seat all the time. I was always talking over my classmates, and... well, the teachers would always say I was just too lively. And that was long before I was even a teenager. So, yes, it's been a part of how I am for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions are motivated by trying to achieve a certain standard or meet expectations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm not really trying to live up to anyone's expectations or reach a certain standard. It's really just... well, like I said, I just have this restless energy. I do things because I need to keep moving, not because I'm trying to prove anything or impress anybody.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does affect things a bit, yes. It can cause some friction with people. Like, at the senior center, I’ve actually had to go up and apologize to folks after our meetings. I just get so going, I guess... and one of my friends even suggested that I take a smaller role at the sign-up table. She told me that my manner was making things tense. I'm not really worried about making mistakes or getting things perfect. It's more that I just... I just like to keep busy. I'm always fixing things, arranging, or volunteering for just one more thing. Even at 87, I... I just have a really hard time sitting down and being still.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy when you're not doing something?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel anxious. I don't get nervous or... or uneasy about it. I just, well, I just prefer to be doing something. It's not really a worried feeling at all, I just don't care to sit idle.\n\nDoctor: Has your need to always be doing something caused any problems for you or your relationships?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, I suppose it has caused a few issues. Um, over at the senior center, I’ve actually had to apologize to folks after our meetings. And... well, one friend even suggested I take a smaller role at the sign-up table. She told me that my manner was just making things a bit tense for everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your constant need to be doing something is a part of who you are and something you like about yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I suppose I've always been a bit of a doer, but... I don't know if I'd say I necessarily like this constant need to be moving around. It's really just what I do. \n\nI like feeling useful, sure, but... well, if it's causing tension with my friends at the center, I can't say I'm exactly thrilled about it. So, no, I wouldn't say it's a part of myself I'm particularly proud of right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself double-checking or making sure things are done perfectly when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't... I don't fuss over making things perfect. Once I get something done or put away, I just leave it be and move on to whatever is next. I'm not really one to go back and double-check things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in charge or have control when you're busy fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel in charge or anything like that. It's more just... I have a lot of energy and I need to do something with it. I'm not doing it to be the boss or to control the situation. I just need to keep my hands busy.\n\nDoctor: Has your tendency to always be doing something been present throughout your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say it has. I remember way back in grade school, I used to get scolded for popping out of my seat all the time. I was always talking over my classmates, and... well, the teachers would always say I was just too lively. And that was long before I was even a teenager. So, yes, it's been a part of how I am for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions are motivated by trying to achieve a certain standard or meet expectations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm not really trying to live up to anyone's expectations or reach a certain standard. It's really just... well, like I said, I just have this restless energy. I do things because I need to keep moving, not because I'm trying to prove anything or impress anybody.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does affect things a bit, yes. It can cause some friction with people. Like, at the senior center, I’ve actually had to go up and apologize to folks after our meetings. I just get so going, I guess... and one of my friends even suggested that I take a smaller role at the sign-up table. She told me that my manner was making things tense. So, yes, it does bother me when it gets in the way of my relationships and makes people uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to avoid making mistakes when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not really worried about making mistakes or getting things perfect. It's more that I just... I just like to keep busy. If I'm fixing something or moving things around, it's just to give my hands something to do, really. It's not about being exact or avoiding errors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your actions are more about being productive or achieving something, rather than just keeping busy?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm retired, so I don't really have any big need to be productive or achieve things anymore. It truly is just about keeping busy. I just... well, I just need to be doing something. It's not like I have a grand goal in mind when I'm puttering around the house or at the center. I just can't seem to sit idle for very long, so I find things to keep my hands and my feet moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is significantly interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned a few minutes ago, it does get in the way sometimes. I don't know if I'd say it causes me severe distress, but it certainly bothers me. Having to go around and apologize to folks after our senior center meetings... well, that's embarrassing. And when my friend told me I should probably step back from the sign-up table because my manner was making everyone tense, that definitely stung. So, yes, it does interfere with my social life and my relationships to some degree.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your constant need to be doing something is something that fits with your self-image?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that, to be honest. I haven't really thought about it in terms of my self-image. It's just... well, it's just how I am. I've just always been someone who needs to keep moving. I don't really sit around pondering if it fits who I am or my identity, it's just what I do every day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to do things better than others when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I'm not trying to compete with anybody or... or show off. It really is just what I said before—I just need something to do. If I see a crooked chair or a stack of magazines that needs straightening, I just fix it because it's there in front of me. It's not about doing it better than someone else, it's just... well, it's just my hands needing to be busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to make things perfect when you're fixing or arranging them?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. She told me that my manner was just making things a bit tense for everyone else.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your constant need to be doing something is a part of who you are and something you like about yourself?\n\nPatient: Well, I haven't really thought about it quite like that. I suppose I've always been a bit of a doer, but... I don't know if I'd say I necessarily like this constant need to be moving around. It's really just what I do. \n\nI like feeling useful, sure, but... well, if it's causing tension with my friends at the center, I can't say I'm exactly thrilled about it. So, no, I wouldn't say it's a part of myself I'm particularly proud of right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself double-checking or making sure things are done perfectly when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don't... I don't fuss over making things perfect. Once I get something done or put away, I just leave it be and move on to whatever is next. I'm not really one to go back and double-check things.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in charge or have control when you're busy fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I feel in charge or anything like that. It's more just... I have a lot of energy and I need to do something with it. I'm not doing it to be the boss or to control the situation. I just need to keep my hands busy.\n\nDoctor: Has your tendency to always be doing something been present throughout your life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I'd say it has. I remember way back in grade school, I used to get scolded for popping out of my seat all the time. So, yes, it's been a part of how I am for as long as I can remember.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your actions are motivated by trying to achieve a certain standard or meet expectations?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I'm not really trying to live up to anyone's expectations or reach a certain standard. It's really just... well, like I said, I just have this restless energy. I do things because I need to keep moving, not because I'm trying to prove anything or impress anybody.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is affecting your overall happiness or well-being?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose it does affect things a bit, yes. It can cause some friction with people. Like, at the senior center, I’ve actually had to go up and apologize to folks after our meetings. I just get so going, I guess... and one of my friends even suggested that I take a smaller role at the sign-up table. She told me that my manner was making things tense. So, yes, it does bother me when it gets in the way of my relationships and makes people uncomfortable.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to avoid making mistakes when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I'm not really worried about making mistakes or getting things perfect. It's more that I just... I just like to keep busy. If I'm fixing something or moving things around, it's just to give my hands something to do, really. It's not about being exact or avoiding errors.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your actions are more about being productive or achieving something, rather than just keeping busy?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I'm retired, so I don't really have any big need to be productive or achieve things anymore. It truly is just about keeping busy. I just... well, I just need to be doing something. It's not like I have a grand goal in mind when I'm puttering around the house or at the center. I just can't seem to sit idle for very long, so I find things to keep my hands and my feet moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your constant need to be doing something is significantly interfering with your daily life or causing you a lot of distress?\n\nPatient: Well, like I mentioned a few minutes ago, it does get in the way sometimes. I don't know if I'd say it causes me severe distress, but it certainly bothers me. Having to go around and apologize to folks after our senior center meetings... well, that's embarrassing. And when my friend told me I should probably step back from the sign-up table because my manner was making everyone tense, that definitely stung. So, yes, it does interfere with my social life and my relationships to some degree.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your constant need to be doing something is something that fits with your self-image?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure how to answer that, to be honest. I haven't really thought about it in terms of my self-image. It's just... well, it's just how I am. I've just always been someone who needs to keep moving. I don't really sit around pondering if it fits who I am or my identity, it's just what I do every day.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to do things better than others when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, not at all. I'm not trying to compete with anybody or... or show off. It really is just what I said before—I just need something to do. It's not about doing it better than someone else, it's just... well, it's just my hands needing to be busy.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to make things perfect when you're fixing or arranging them?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say that. I just do it because I have a hard time just sitting perfectly still with nothing to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your constant need to be doing something is something you would like to change about yourself?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean... I've been this way for a long time, and it doesn't really bother me. It doesn't cause any real trouble in my life. Like I said, it just keeps me busy. So, no, I don't think it's something I'd want to change at this point.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your actions are driven by a need to have things a certain way?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I just like to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're trying to exert control over your environment when you're fixing or arranging things?\n\nPatient: No, I don't feel like I'm trying to control anything. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I wouldn't say it's ruined anything, but... it has caused a few bumps, I suppose. At the senior center, I've had to apologize a few times after our committee meetings because I guess I was just too restless. And... well, one of my friends there actually suggested I take a smaller role at the sign-up table. She said the way I was acting was making things a bit tense for folks coming in. So, it does get in the way a little bit, but I still go and participate.
